| **I-92** | IML is a real step and `ARCHMODE` is it; `ARCHLVL` is a different Hercules | Mike asked where the IML procedure lives, and the answer reframes something the project had been doing implicitly. On real iron the service processor performs IML — Initial Microprogram Load — before any IPL, and on a 308x that is what decided whether the machine came up S/370 or 370-XA; it was not changeable at runtime, one re-IML'd. PR/SM later made it the LPAR activation profile's mode. The only runtime architecture switch that has ever existed in the family is ESA/390 to z/Architecture via `SIGP set-architecture`; there is **no architected way for S/370 software to elect ESA/390**. So there is nothing to write into CP, and the constraint the project has been living under without naming it is simply this: **the CP build must match the mode the machine was IML'd in**, which is exactly why the card-deck build IPL stays S/370 while the disk IPL is ESA/390 (`I-83`). In Hercules the IML is the `ARCHMODE` statement, and it is also a runtime panel command — `archmode [S/370 | ESA/390 | z/Arch | ESAME]` — an operator action outside the guest. **`ARCHLVL` is Hercules 4.x and does not exist here.** Two corrections fell out of checking rather than assuming: this machine runs **Hercules 3.13**, not the 3.07 that `STATE.md` states as fact in a document headed "read this first"; and every run so far has used `ipl`, which is Load **Normal**, where `iplc` is Load **Clear** — a fresh emulator process starts with zeroed storage so it has not bitten us, but load-clear is the truer analogue of a machine fresh from IML and costs nothing. | fixed |
| **I-93** | `TSCH` cc1 is not S/370 "device available" — it is also "still running" | The instruction trace ended the guesswork. `t 800-1800` plus `t+` over the bootstrap showed the whole chain in twenty lines: a `TSCH` returning cc1, `XAIO` mapping it to cc0, the caller reading a CSW that was never built, a `TM` setting cc3 (**not** an I/O condition code — the cc3 I had been chasing came from a `TM`, which would have cost hours without the trace), a branch to CP's own `MSG900W` program-check handler, `CHKERR` saving state and trying to print, and `L R15,DMKOPRWT` / `BALR R14,R15` through a zero vector into absolute 0. Two defects, one of them mine. **Mine**: `TSCH` cc1 means "IRB stored, subchannel not status-pending", which is the answer both for an idle subchannel and for one with an operation still in flight. `XAIO` read it as S/370 `TIO` cc0, *device available* — the literal equivalent and the wrong answer, because CP polls with `TIO 0(R2)` / `BC 6,*-4`, looping on cc1 and cc2 and **leaving on cc0**, draining status until the device is idle and then testing the CSW of the last status stored. In S/370 a device with an operation in flight answers `TIO` with cc2, busy; in ESA/390 that same subchannel is simply not status-pending. So the loop left on the first poll, before the I/O finished. The SCSW says which case it is — function control and activity control are non-zero while a function is in progress — so `XAIOTIOF` now tests `XAIOIRB+2,X'7F'` and `XAIOIRB+3,X'E0'` and returns cc2 when either is set, using the `LA`/`LTR` idiom the macro already documents for cc2 against the `LA`/`LCR` it uses for cc1. Fixed identically in `XAIOB`. **This is the first defect in the conversion that is a genuine semantic difference rather than a rename**, which is exactly where the `XAIO` header warned the risk was: the condition-code contract. | fixed |
| **I-94** | `DMKCKP`'s error printer calls through a vector nothing fills | Latent CP behaviour, exposed rather than caused by this conversion, and recorded because it will keep hiding root causes. `DMKCKP` ends with a table of **`DS` fields** — `DMKOPRWT`, `DMKSYSOW`, `DMKSYSOC`, `DMKSYSTP`, `DMKSYSWM`, `DMKTMRPT`, `DMKSYSRM` — each commented `V(name)` but declared `DS 1F`, so they reserve four bytes, emit no object text and generate no RLD. Measured in the deck: `DMKCKP`'s text ends at offset X'16A6' and the field the failing instruction loads is at X'16E8', past the end, so the loader never touches it. Nothing in `DMKCKP` stores into them; the module even carries a self-modifying trick at 00852000 (`ORG *-4` then `DC X'50'` to turn an `L` into an `ST`) that copies **from** the `DS` field **into** the literal pool, which only makes sense if the `DS` is authoritative and filled from outside. In the bootstrap, before whatever fills it has run, `L R15,DMKOPRWT` / `BALR R14,R15` branches to zero. The consequence for debugging: **any error inside `DMKCKPT` becomes a wait-state crash rather than a message**, so the first failure is always invisible and the second one is what you see. Not fixed — fixing it means establishing who fills that table in a normal startup, which is real work on a path that only runs once something else is already wrong. Use the instruction trace instead. | open |
| **I-95** | A shadow restore rewinds the staged update decks too, so a rebuild silently uses old ones | `I-90` established that restoring a shadow set rewinds CP's spool queue along with the disk. It also rewinds **the update decks staged on MAINT's A-disk**, and that is worse, because the spool problem announces itself as a deck of the wrong size while this one produces a clean full assembly of the wrong source. After `XAIO`/`XAIOB` were fixed, the cycle restored `shadows.cms` to get a bootable CMS, re-read the two macros, rebuilt the MACLIB and ran a full `ASMDMK` — **185 clean, 1 flagged**, the flagged one being `I-34`'s pre-existing `DMKRIO`. Every signal said success. But the snapshot predated both the `I-86` placement fix and the `I-88` IPL-device fix, and `DMKCKP.XA0015DK` was never re-read, so `DMKCKP` assembled against the **old** deck. The IPL went straight back to the `I-86` symptom — `STM 14,1,1556(13)` and `BAL 14,1874(0,13)` reaching X'1E14' and X'1F52', both above X'1000' — with `GR02`/`GR10` back to zero because `I-88` was gone as well. The rule that follows: **after a shadow restore, re-read every deck that changed since the snapshot, or refresh the snapshot instead.** More generally this is the third time a snapshot restore has silently reverted something outside the thing being restored (spool, assemblies, now decks); the safe framing is that a restore rewinds *the whole volume set* and the burden is on the caller to re-establish everything it was relying on. The gate now checks all three `DMKCKP` fixes by byte pattern — `I-86` placement, `I-88`'s `L R1,IOSSID`, and `I-93`'s two `TM`s — so no single stale input can pass it. | fixed |
| **I-96** | `DMKSAV` *is* on the disk-IPL path — I-83 was half right | `I-83` concluded that the card loader and `DMKSAV` are build-time only and that a disk IPL touches neither, and on that basis `DMKSAV`'s conversion was reverted so the card-deck build would keep working. The first half holds: `DMKLD00E` really is card-only. The second half is wrong, and the machine said so plainly. The ESA/390 IPL now reaches an operation exception with `SAVEPSW` = `000C0000 00070020` and `SAVECODE` = `00040001`, and storage at the failing address reads `C4D4D2E2 C1E54040` — **"DMKSAV  "** — with `9C00A000`, `SIO 0(R10)`, four bytes earlier. `DMKCKP` 00354000 is `GOTO DMKSAVRS  START SYSTEM RE-IPL`: the saved nucleus hands control to `DMKSAV`'s restart entry, so `DMKSAV` runs on **both** paths. That creates a genuine conflict rather than an oversight to correct. The build executes `DMKSAV` on an S/370 machine — proven by `Nucleus loaded on VM50-1 --- Starting CYL=0530`, which is `DMKSAVNC` writing SYSRES — while the disk IPL executes it on an ESA/390 one. One object deck cannot satisfy both with static opcodes. Three ways out, none free: convert `DMKSAV` and move the build to ESA/390, which drags in `DMKLD00E` (`I-80`) or requires an already-working ESA/390 CP to build under, which is circular; make `DMKSAV`'s I/O **mode-aware at runtime**, probing once and setting a switch, which is the classic answer and is how CP already detects the ECPS:VM assists; or build with S/370 `DMKSAV` and patch the written nucleus afterwards, which is the ugly one. **The methodological point is the one to keep**: `I-83` was reasoning about a control path from source reading, and it was half wrong in the half that had no evidence behind it — the loader claim was tested, the `DMKSAV` claim was inferred. **Closed 30 September.** Acted on. The third option it listed -- make `DMKSAV`'s I/O mode-aware at runtime -- was implemented as the architecture probe (`I-98`), and `I-100` then fixed the probe result being baked into the written nucleus. `DMKSAV` now serves both architectures from one object deck. | closed |
| **I-97** | An update deck must not anchor inside a multi-statement constant, and a macro must return the alignment it borrowed | Adding the architecture probe to `XAIO` made `DMKCKP` fail with `IFO220 ALIGNMENT ERROR` on `LPSW WAIT17` at 00349100 — a statement this conversion never touched, hundreds of lines from anything edited, and clean in every previous build. Two distinct defects, and the first was already live and silent. **`WAIT16` is an eight-byte PSW assembled from two four-byte `DC`s**: `X'000A0000'` at 00517500 and `X'00000016'` at 00517600. `./ I 00517500` inserts *after the first of them*, so `XAIOWORK` had been sitting **inside the PSW**, splitting it — and `MVC IPLPSW(8),WAIT16` at 00234600 was copying four bytes of `WAIT16` followed by four bytes of work area into the restart PSW. Nothing complained, because the assembler has no opinion about what an `MVC` reaches. It only surfaced when the probe changed `XAIOWORK`'s length by an odd number of words and knocked the *neighbouring* `WAIT17` off the doubleword that `LPSW` requires — **the diagnostic named the neighbour, not the victim**. Anchor moved to 00517600, past both halves. Second: a macro that emits storage decides the alignment of whatever the module declares next, so `XAIOWORK` now ends with `DS 0D` and hands back the location counter it was given. This is `I-77`'s family a third time — `I-77` expanded inside a DSECT, `I-86` expanded above an IPL read boundary, and this one expanded inside a constant — and the common rule is the same: **a sequence number is not a safe anchor until you have looked at what it is in the middle of**. | fixed |
| **I-98** | `XAIO` now probes the architecture, so one object deck is correct on both machines | `I-96` left a genuine conflict: `DMKSAV` runs on the build path (an S/370 machine — that is what writes SYSRES) and on the disk-IPL path (ESA/390), and static opcodes cannot satisfy both. Resolved by making the macro ask the machine, which is CP's own idiom — `DMKCPI` detects the ECPS:VM assists by executing one and recovering from the program check. `XAIOPROB` saves the caller's program-new PSW, points it at a recovery label, executes `STSCH` (ESA/390-only; a S/370 takes an operation exception), and restores; landing normally means ESA/390, landing in the recovery means S/370. Each of `XAIOSIO`/`XAIOTIO`/`XAIOHIO` then takes the native path — in S/370 simply issuing the original instruction, since R1 already holds the device address, the hardware stores the CSW at X'40' itself and the condition codes are the ones the caller was written for. **Both halves are proven by execution.** The build ran the *converted* `DMKSAV` on a S/370 machine and succeeded — `Nucleus loaded on VM50-1 --- Starting CYL=0530`, wait X'012' — so object code containing `SSCH` built a nucleus on a machine with no `SSCH`. The ESA/390 IPL of the same deck shows `STSCH` executing, no S/370 `SIO`/`TIO` taken anywhere, and `SSCH` in use. The approach also pre-solves `I-80` should the card path ever need ESA/390. | fixed |
| **I-99** | The failure moves on: the nucleus-cylinder read exhausts its retries | With the architecture probe (`I-98`) and the `TSCH` busy fix (`I-93`) both in place the ESA/390 IPL gets materially further, and the trace shows it rather than implying it: `CLC CSW+4(2)` against `CE+DE` now **passes**, where in the earlier trace the CSW had never been built at all. The converted I/O completes correctly. The new stop is a flag test rather than an instruction — `TM 2100(12),128` then `BC 1,872(0,12)`, which is `DMKCKP` 00346000/00347000, `TM FLAG,CKPLD` / `BO RESTAUT`, falling into `B MSG902N  FATAL IO ERROR ON NUCLEUS CYL`. `BO` is `BC 1` and `CKPLD` is the X'80' bit. So control reaches `TESTP2` with the hundred-retry count exhausted while reading **the nucleus cylinder**, with `FLAG` saying `DMKSAV` is what is being loaded. The checkpoint page loads, the bootstrap runs, and the read of the nucleus itself fails repeatedly — a later and different problem than anything seen before. Pinning the exact statement wants the assembly listing, which maps the location counter to source; `VMFASM` writes `$DMKCKP LISTING` to **disk** by default (`I-32`) rather than the printer, and the printer queues at shutdown exactly like the punch (`I-78`), so it is `PRINT $DMKCKP LISTING A` plus a drain — two runs, and worth doing once because it turns every future offset-to-source step from pattern matching into a lookup. **Closed 30 September.** Superseded. The nucleus-cylinder read succeeds; the IPL now runs through `DMKSAV`, `DMKCKP` and most of `DMKCPI` before failing elsewhere. Retained for the method note, which still holds. | closed |
| **I-100** | `DMKSAV` bakes its own probe result into the nucleus it writes | `I-98`'s architecture probe caches its answer in `XAIOMODE`, which is the right design everywhere except here. `DMKSAV` is the one module whose **runtime state ends up in the nucleus it produces**: the build runs it on a real S/370, so the probe caches `X'01'`, and `DMKSAVNC` then writes the nucleus to disk *including its own data area*. The ESA/390 IPL reads that back and trusts it. The trace is unambiguous — `CLI XAIOMODE,X'00'` finds it already non-zero and skips the probe, `CLI XAIOMODE,X'01'` says S/370, and `SIO 0(1)` issues on an ESA/390 machine. Fixed with one `MVI XAIOMODE,X'00'` at entry, placed **after** sequence 00145000 rather than before it, because `STCAW` is a branch target — `BC 15,8(0,3)` reaches it from `DMKSAVRS` — and anything ahead of the label is skipped on that path. **The general shape is worth keeping: a cache is unsafe in any module that serialises itself.** | closed |
| **I-101** | The staged file set could not rebuild itself from a fresh extraction | `VMFMAC` failed with `*** XAOPS COPY OR MACRO NOT FOUND ***` on the first clean rebuild after the container was reclaimed. `XAOPS.MACRO` — the member defining the ESA/390 opcodes — lives in `arch/31bit/macros/`, not in `arch/31bit/updates/` where `mkrun.py` resolves `read:` specs. It had been read onto MAINT's A-disk by an early run and simply persisted there across every later one, so **every run for days was building on state no script could recreate**, and nothing noticed because nothing ever needed to. The reclaim exposed it for the price of one twelve-minute run. Fixed by symlinking the member into `updates/` so there is still one source of truth. CE behaved well: the failed build went to `NEWMAC MACLIB` and left `DMKLCL MACLIB` untouched. **The lesson is that a green run proves the artifact, not the procedure** — reproducibility is only tested by destroying the workspace, and that test had never been run. | closed |
| **I-102** | `DMKSAV` builds an EC-mode system with BC-mode code | The ESA/390 IPL's specification exception (`SAVECODE` = `00000006`, **ILC 0**) is `LPSW XWAIT` at module offset X'32'. The trace names the constant: `R:00071220 = 7E060000 00071036`, whose byte 1 `X'06'` has **bit 12 = 0** — a BC-mode enabled-wait PSW. ESA/390 requires bit 12 = 1, and an exception recognised while *loading* a PSW is reported with ILC 0, which is what made the code identifiable. The module states the contradiction itself: `IPLDATA DC X'000C000000000800'  EXTENDED PSW FOR IPL` — the PSW `DMKSAV` **writes** is EC mode, and is why CP runs in EC mode at all (`I-82`); all eight PSWs it **loads** were BC mode. The one that mattered most was not the failing one: `DISAWT0` is wait code **012**, `NUCLEUS LOAD ON 'LABEL'` — the success gate every run in this project tests — so a fully working conversion would have program-checked at the finishing line and read as the I/O still being broken. Seven constants were one nibble; `XWAIT` was not, because `X'7E'` sets PSW bits 2-4, BC-mode channel masks that **must be zero in EC mode**, so bit 12 alone would have traded one specification exception for another. `DMKCKP`, converted by IBM at `@V407429`, already carries the right constant for the same job (`IWAIT DC X'020E0000'`). Both enabled waits were replaced with `XATIO` polls rather than repaired: the interrupt path would need four coupled changes — EC-mode PSW, a **CR6** subclass mask (`DMKSAV` sets **CR2**, the S/370 channel-mask register, and the trace shows CR6 = 0, so a corrected `XWAIT` would have waited for ever), the device test moved off the I/O old PSW's X'3A' field, and status moved from the CSW to `TSCH` — whereas polling needs none, is what the rest of the module already does, and `XATIO`'s CSW shim keeps every following `CLC CSW+4(2)` working. **Unlike the I/O instructions this fix needs no probe: S/370 supports BC and EC mode, ESA/390 only EC, so EC mode is correct unconditionally on both.** **Closed 30 September.** Fixed in `110ff14` and extended to the rest of the IPL path by `I-107`. All eight of `DMKSAV`'s PSW constants are EC mode, and the S/370 build still reaches wait X'012' -- now reported as `000A0000 00000012` rather than `00020000 00000012`, which is the converted `DISAWT0` proving the change is correct on both machines. | closed |
| **I-104** | CP sizes real storage with `SSK`, which ESA/390 does not have — and the failure is silent | `DMKCPI` at `SLICEOK` (00588000) measures main storage by pointing the program-new PSW at `CPIPINT` and then walking `SSK R2,R5` upward in 2 KB steps until it takes an addressing exception; `R5` at the exception is the size, stored into `DMKSYSRM`. **`SSK` is not provided in 370-XA or ESA/390** — the PoP's Appendix F comparison gives it `-` against `B` for `SSKE`, as it does for `RRB` against `RRBE`. So the *first* `SSK` takes an **operation** exception, which lands on `CPIPINT` — the loop's own designed exit — and stores `R5` = **0**. CP believes it has zero bytes of real storage, and nothing reports anything. Ten steps follow, each verified in the trace: `DMKSYSRV` (16 MB, correct) > `DMKSYSRM` (0) so `OFFLOOP` marks all 4,096 pages offline and walks `R3` down to `ACORETBL-16`; `SL R11,ACORETBL` is a SUBTRACT LOGICAL, so 16 below the base wraps to `FFFFFFF0`; `>>4` gives `0FFFFFFF`, `+1` gives `10000000`; `FREBEE` turns that into `R11` = `00FFFFFF`; `SETUP` computes the loop limit as top - (R11 x 16), which is negative; and `BXH` compares **signed**, so `FRELOOP` never terminates and writes the literal `C'FREE'` across the nucleus until it overwrites its own `BXH` — at which point the operation exception that finally surfaces is ILC 3 on data, 4,096 pages too late to mean anything. **The scheduling consequence is the important part.** `23-STORAGE-KEYS.md` placed the storage-key family *third*, behind the `INTTIO` group and the bootstrap chain, on the correct reasoning that the 24-bit masking limits **real** storage and so gates milestone C rather than a 31-bit CMS. That reasoning still holds for 66 of the 67 sites. It does not hold for this one: **a single `SSK`, used as a measuring instrument rather than to set a key, is a Stage-1 IPL blocker.** An axis can be correctly deprioritised in aggregate and still contain one site on the critical path — which is an argument for asking of every deferred axis *which single site runs earliest*, not merely how large the axis is. | fixed |
| **I-105** | The work-area anchor rule was too narrow: it is the first DSECT-opening `COPY`, not the `PSA` macro | `I-77` concluded that `XAIOWORK` must be anchored *before* the `PSA` macro, because `./ I` inserts AFTER its anchor and expanding the work area inside `PSA DSECT` makes every subroutine an offset into low storage that generates no object code at all. Applying that rule to `DMKOPR` put the anchor at `COPY EQU` (00277000) and earned **81 `IFO209 ADDRESSABILITY ERROR`**. The module's tail is `LTORG` (00274000), `EJECT` (00275000), `COPY RBLOKS` (00276000), `COPY EQU`, `COPY DEVTYPES`, `PSA`, `END`. **`COPY RBLOKS` opens `RCHBLOK DSECT` and never returns to the CSECT**, so everything after it is already in a dummy section and the `PSA` macro is merely the last of several wrong anchors, not the first. The rule is therefore **anchor before the first `COPY` that opens a DSECT**; in `DMKSAV` that happened to be the same statement as the `PSA` macro, which is why the narrower formulation survived. Fixed by anchoring at the `EJECT` at 00275000, the last statement still in the CSECT. **The generalisable point is that a rule derived from one instance states the instance, not the rule** — `I-77` was right about `DMKSAV` and wrong about the principle, and the failure mode differed too (`I-77` was silent, this one was 81 diagnostics). | closed |
| **I-106** | `XAIO` borrowed R15, which is a `USING` base in 54 of CP's 201 modules | With `DMKOPR` assembling clean the ESA/390 IPL reached interruption code **X'15'**, and `SA22-7201-08` is unambiguous about what that is: *"An operand exception is recognized when … START/STORE/TEST SUBCHANNEL is attempted, and bits 0-15 of general register 1 do not contain 0001 hex."* `XAIO` used **R15** twice — as the subchannel scan counter in `XAIOFIND` and as the condition-code return value — and `DMKOPR` is `USING DMKOPRWT,R15`. Every field in the work area resolves through that same base, so `SR R15,R15` at the top of the search loop destroyed it, the loop's `XAIOPFX`, `XAIOSCHB` and `XAIODEV` references addressed low storage instead, and the subsystem ID handed to `STSCH` was whatever happened to be there. **This was latent in eleven of the nineteen modules already converted** — `DMKCPI`, `DMKDSP`, `DMKIOS`, `DMKIOT`, `DMKFRE`, `DMKCFO` and `DMKCNS` among them — and had never fired only because `DMKSAV`, the module driving every test so far, is based on R3. No substitute register exists: counting `USING` operands across the tree shows **all sixteen registers are a base somewhere in CP** (R0 175 modules, R11 159, R13 154, R15 54), and R0 is separately reserved because every caller has `USING PSA,R0` — the macro already recorded that an earlier draft tried R0 and failed. So the fix borrows none. **R1 becomes its own counter**, because the subsystem ID is the prefix in the high halfword and the subchannel number in the low, so incrementing it *is* stepping to the next subchannel; and **all four condition codes are now set from storage** by generalising the `TM XAIOFF,X'FF'` trick the macro already used for cc3 — `TM XAIO00,X'FF'` cc0, `CLI XAIO00,X'01'` cc1, `CLI XAIOFF,X'01'` cc2. `XAIOB` was checked and does **not** need the same change: its work area is IOBLOK-relative rather than CSECT-relative, and its R15 writes all sit immediately before a `BR R14` with no base-relative reference after them — which is also the precise reason the same clobber is fatal in `XAIOFIND` and harmless there. | fixed |
| **I-107** | `I-102` was fixed in one module when it was a class across several | `I-102` converted `DMKSAV`'s eight BC-mode PSW constants to EC mode and stopped there, because `DMKSAV` was the only module whose PSWs the IPL had yet reached. Sweeping the whole IPL path afterwards found the rest: **`DMKCPI`'s six disabled-wait PSWs** — `XWAIT1`, `XWAIT2`, `XWAIT3`, `XWAIT9`, `XWAIT4`, `XWAITCSS`, all `X'0002...'` — and **`DMKCCH`'s `WAITCCH`**, same shape. These are CP's error exits: *CAN'T GET TO CONSOLES*, *REAL MACHINE TOO SMALL FOR VM/370*, *FAILED TO CONNECT CHANNELS*. Leaving them BC mode does not merely risk a fault later — it means **every initialisation failure reports as a specification exception instead of its own wait code**, so CP loses the ability to say what is wrong at exactly the moment it is trying to. The `CHANWT` PSW sitting beside them at 01986000 is already `X'020A...'`: IBM converted some of this module's PSWs and not others, the same mixed state `DMKCKP` was in, which is why a mechanical sweep beats reading. Fixing these did **not** change the observed failure, which is worth recording as a negative result: they were genuinely broken and are now correct, but CP was not reaching them. Two process notes. The sweep reads **base source**, so `DMKSAV`'s eight still appear unconverted even though `XA0017DK` fixes them at update level — a scan of source cannot see an UPDATE level, and reporting its output as a defect list would have overstated the work by eight sites. And the first version of the replacement dropped the labels: an UPDATE replacement is a whole card, so emitting `DC X'000A...'` without `XWAIT1` in columns 1-8 would have left every `LPSW XWAIT1` unresolved. | closed |
| **I-108** | `DMKCPI` reads a volume label from every device except the IPL device | With `I-87` fixed, `MOUNTALL` brings devices online and reads labels from **sixteen** of them -- `6A0 6A2 6A3 6A4 6A5 6A6 6A7 6A8 6AC 6AD 6B0 6B1 6D0 6D1 6D2 6D3` -- and stores each volume serial with `MVC RDEVSER,0(R14)`. **`6A1` is not among them**, and `6A1` is the only one that matters: `DMKSYS` declares `SYSRES=6A1, SYSVOL=VM50-1`, so `DMKCPI` at 01469000 then calls `DMKSCNVS` looking for `VM50-1`, finds no device carrying it, and ends at `ABEND CPI001` -- *SYSRES DEVICE NOT FOUND OR INCORRECT*. CP reads a label from every device in the configuration except the one it is about to go looking for. The suspect is the thing that makes `6A1` different: the IPL leaves its subchannel with status pending, or busy, from the nucleus read, so the device probe sees status rather than 'available' and the retry gives up into `MOUNTDVI`. It is also the one device for which the `I-87` fix **skips** the `MSCH`, since IPL already enabled it -- so it is the least-exercised path through the new code. Not yet diagnosed; the next step is to trace the device-probe gates with `R15` = `6A1` and see which branch takes it out. **Fixed 30 September, and the cause was mine.** Not a property of the IPL device at all: `XAIOPROB`, the architecture probe added by `I-98`, saves R14 in `XAIOSB2` but **not R1** -- and R1 is the caller's device number, which the probe needs for its own `L R1,XAIOPFX`. The trace shows it directly: after the probe returns, `CH R1,XAIODEV` and then `STH R1,XAIODEV` run with R1 = `X'00010000'`, so `XAIOFIND` stores `X'0000'` as the device it is hunting and scans 256 subchannels for a PMCW that cannot exist. It returns **cc3, not operational**, for a device that is present and healthy. **The probe runs exactly once per module**, so it claims exactly one victim, chosen purely by call order -- which is why this presented as a property of `6A1` and why `6A0` and `6A2` were unaffected. Fixed by stashing R1 in a new `XAIOSB3` across the probe. `XAIOB` has no probe and needs nothing. **Two method notes.** The register values in the trace extract come from Hercules' periodic dumps rather than per-instruction, so *"the victim is 6A1"* was inferred while the clobber itself was observed; the rebuild settled it. And the user's instinct -- *mount SYSRES on another address* -- was right that the address mattered, for a reason neither of us had: not the device, the call order. | closed |
| **I-110** | `PRG018` was `SSK` at `KEYLOOP`, and CP sizes storage by *expecting* a program check -- so the failure is not merely silent, it is indistinguishable from success | `I-104` converted `DMKCPI`'s `SSK` to `SSKE` on the strength of the instruction being illegal. What it actually caused was never observed, and it is worth recording in full, because the mechanism is a trap in CP's design rather than a bug in ours. `DMKCPI` at `SLICEOK` (00588000) measures real storage by pointing the program-new PSW at its own `CPIPINT` label and then walking upward setting keys until an addressing exception stops it; the exception **is** the normal exit, and `R5` at that moment is the answer. `LPSW PRNPSW` at 00597500 reaches the same label on a machine that never faults. So an **operation exception on the very first `SSK`** lands at `CPIPINT` with `R5` still zero, and `ST R5,0(,R3)` records the real machine size as **zero**. Everything after that is arithmetic, and every step was read out of a stopped machine: `DMKSYSRM` = 0 makes `R5` (the real-storage top-of-table) one entry *below* `ACORETBL`, so `OFFLOOP` flags all 4096 pages `*OL*` -- the marker is still sitting at `DMKSYSCS`; `FPDFLT` then computes `R11 = R3 - ACORETBL` = -16, and **`SRL R11,4` is a *logical* shift**, which turns -16 into `0FFFFFFF`; `+1`, then `FREBEE`'s `SH 64 / SRL 4 / LA 3(,R11)` gives `R11` = `00FFFFFF`; `SLL R6,4` gives `0FFFFFF0`; `SR R5,R6` gives `F00407B8`, which is **negative**, and `BXH` compares signed, so `FRELOOP` has no exit. It then walks down out of the CORTABLE, through the nucleus, and **overwrites its own `BXH` instruction** with the `C'FREE'` it is storing -- leaving opcode `C6` at `X'6A388'`, ILC 3, operation exception, and a hardware program-interrupt loop once the program-new PSW at `X'68'` is among the casualties. Measured: `GR03` = `000407A8`, `GR11` = `00FFFFFF`, `GR05` = `F00407B8`, `ACORETBL` = `000407B8`, and `0825` (`SSK R2,R5`) still in storage at `X'6A17E'`. **Three things this settles.** (1) `PRG018` is explained, which is what `I-109` was for. (2) **The `MAINSIZE` misattribution is dead.** The FRELOOP runaway was blamed on `MAINSIZE 256`, then seen at 16 MB and left unexplained; it was never about storage size, because the size CP uses is one it derives, and it derived zero. (3) A 16-byte error becomes a 256 MB one through one logical shift, so `SRL` on a value that can go negative is worth a sweep of its own. | fixed by `I-104` |
| **I-111** | A snapshot is a *timestamp*, and reassembling one module against an older one silently ships stale modules | `SNAP-BASE` was taken at 07:20 on 30 Sep. `I-104` (09:01), `I-106` (11:12), `I-107` (13:59), `I-87` (15:15) and `I-108` (16:39) all landed after it, and three of those are in `XAIO.MACRO`, which **every** converted module expands. The `I-109` build restored `SNAP-BASE` and reassembled `DMKDMP` alone, reasoning that the MACLIB was unchanged -- true relative to the previous build, false relative to the snapshot being restored. The result was a 07:20 nucleus carrying one 18:40 module, and it reproduced the *pre-`I-104`* failure convincingly enough that the first reading of it was "the failure moved". Caught only by decoding the running nucleus and finding `0825` where `SSKE` should have been. This is `I-101` again -- building on state no script recreates -- and `c7.sh` already carried the rule: *restore, re-read every deck that postdates the snapshot, rebuild.* **Fix, in the artifact rather than the habit:** the full rebuild now takes a fresh `SNAP-CUR` immediately after the assembly and before the nucleus write, so the snapshot a one-module cycle restores is current by construction. Related near-miss, fixed in the same pass: `mkrun` failing did not abort the driver, which fell through and IPLed the *previous* run's `.rc` -- a plausible log from a run nobody configured. | fixed |
| **I-115** | Three diagnostic runs in a row were read as evidence while running in the wrong architecture | A build driver sets `ARCHMODE ESA/390` for its test phase and resets it to `S/370` on exit, which is correct -- the next build needs S/370. Three diagnostics afterwards forgot to set it back, and under S/370 the converted nucleus dies on its first converted instruction. **The symptom is not an error.** It is `PSW=00000000 40000000` -- CPU stopped, no PSW loaded -- and an instruction trace containing *nothing*, which reads exactly like "execution never reaches this range" and was taken for that twice: once to conclude `GR12`/`GR13`/`GR14` pointing into DMKDMP were stale leftovers, and once to conclude CP never executes in DMKCPI's range. Both conclusions were wrong; a later ESA/390 trace of the same range returned **3,533,909 instructions**, and the registers were live. **Fix in the artifact:** `mkrun.arch_of(log)` and `mkrun.wrong_arch(log, want)` read the mode back out of Hercules's own `architecture mode` line, and `diag.sh` sets ESA/390 and then *verifies* it before any output is believed. The general rule this is the fourth instance of: **the only trustworthy statement about a run is one the run itself made.** The other three were the stale nucleus (`I-111`), the fall-through `.rc` that IPLed the previous run's script, and the swallowed `CP DISC` (`I-112`). | fixed |
| **I-116** | `RBLOKS.XA0002DK` grew the RDEVBLOK DSECT and left the macro that BUILDS the blocks at the old length | **This is the cause of `CPI001`,** and it is a defect this project introduced. `XA0002DK` inserts `RDEVSSID` plus a reserved fullword after `RDEVIOBL`, the DSECT's last field, so `RDEVSIZE EQU (*-RDEVBLOK)/8` went from 11 doublewords to 12. But DMKRIO's blocks are not cut from the DSECT: `RDEVICE.MACRO` emits them as a list of explicit `DC` statements and ends at `DC F'0' RESERVED FOR IBM USE`, which *is* `RDEVIOBL`. The array stayed at 88 bytes a block while every scanner began striding 96 -- `DMKSCNVS` is a flat array scan, `LA R1,RDEVSIZE*8(,R1)`, not a chain walk -- so it reads 8 bytes further out per block, cannot match `RDEVSER` against `DMKSYSVL`, and DMKCPI takes `ABEND 1 -- SYSRES DEVICE NOT FOUND OR INCORRECT`. **Measured, not argued.** `tools/dumpscan.py` reads the printed abend dump back into a byte image: `ARIODV = 0149E8`, count `949`, observed stride `X'58'`, and `0149E8 + 949 x X'58' = 029020`, which is `ARIOCU`, the first RCUBLOK -- **the arrays abut exactly**, so the stride is settled beyond coincidence. Over 949 blocks the error accumulates to 7592 bytes, so the scan is not slightly misaligned, it is past the end of the array. The dump *does* contain an RDEVBLOK holding `VM50-1` at about `X'21CF8'`; the data was there and the stride was not, which is why this looked for a while like "the label was never stored". **Why nothing caught it.** Both halves are correct in isolation, they live in different files, and neither mentions the other, so all 201 modules assemble with `NO STATEMENTS FLAGGED`. The deck's own comment reads *"RESERVED, KEEPS RDEVSIZE EXACT"* -- care was taken that `RDEVSIZE` stayed a whole number of doublewords, and the question of whether anything *built* blocks to a different length was never asked. **Standing rule: adding a field to a DSECT is only half a change if a macro generates that block's storage.** **Rejected alternative:** `ORG`-ing `RDEVSSID` onto `RDEVIOBL`, unused under AP=NO (`I-50`), would need no generator change and no length change anywhere -- refused because it overloads a field a future AP revival needs, and `dmksys()` deliberately keeps that door open. Eight bytes a block is the cheaper debt. **And a tool withdrawn.** The first attempt at a class-level check parsed both lengths statically and reported all three of RDEVBLOK, RCUBLOK and RCHBLOK as disagreeing -- two of them wrongly. Its own output said why: `RDEVICE.MACRO` holds **339 `AIF`/`AGO` statements**, so a static walk of its `DC`s sums every conditional path at once. It was deleted rather than shipped: a tool that reports three defects where there is one would have sent the next reader to fix RCHBLOK. `dumpscan.py` measures the artifact instead. | fix known |
| **I-117** | A wait for Hercules to exit that returned success on timeout, and started a second instance on the same pack | `run()` waited with `w(){ for i in $(seq 1 900); do test "$(pgrep -c hercules)" = "0" && return 0; sleep 3; done; }` -- a 45-minute ceiling that, on timeout, **falls out of the loop and returns the exit status of `sleep`, which is 0.** So a wait that gave up was indistinguishable from a wait that succeeded. Phase 1 of the I-116 build was still staging at 45 minutes (58 members, not 48), the driver concluded it was finished, **took a snapshot from shadow files that were open and being written**, and started a SECOND Hercules on the same pack -- `I-63`'s run45 hazard, reproduced by a check that could not fail. Damage, checked rather than assumed: the second instance logged `HHCCD130E 06A1 file[1] read incomplete` and `console: DBG026: bind: Address already in use`, so it never bound its console and its script never reached `VMFLOAD` or `CP IPL 00C` -- no nucleus was written and no second writer was established. It wedged, `SIGTERM` did not take, and it was killed; justified because it had done no work to flush, which is the only thing the clean-shutdown rule protects. The snapshot was discarded and re-taken with Hercules down. Phase 1 itself survived and completed: `vmfmac` clean, `asmdmk` `T=24.83/47.67`, 185 of 186 clean. **Three fixes, because one is not the class.** `w()` now returns failure after 90 minutes; `run()` aborts when the wait fails; and `run()` **refuses to start while a Hercules is already running**. The third is the only real guard -- no timeout value prevents two instances on one pack, only a check does. Fifth instance in one day of the same shape: a check that cannot fail (`I-111` stale snapshot, the `mkrun` fall-through, `I-112` swallowed `CP DISC`, `I-115` wrong architecture, this). | fixed |
| **I-118** | `PRG018` is a translation-specification exception: CP's segment table is S/370 format and `LRA` rejects it | With `I-116` fixed, `DMKSCNVS` finds SYSRES, DMKCPI runs past the volume check, and CP fails later and differently. **`PRG018` decodes literally**: `r 8C` gives `00040012` -- ILC 2, so a four-byte instruction, and interruption code `X'12'`, which is 18 decimal and is a **translation-specification exception**. The dump names the cause in one line. `CR1 = 05FFC840` is the segment-table designation, and storage at `X'FFC840'` holds `F00561D0 F00562A8 F0056380 F0056458 ...` -- CP's segment table, in **System/370 format**: the high nibble `F` is `SEGPLEN`, a page-table length of 16 pages for a 64 KB segment. Under ESA/390 an STE's bits 0-3 are part of the page-table origin and the length lives in bits 28-31, and **bit 0 is unassigned and must be zero** -- it is 1 in every entry. That is a translation-specification exception by definition, and it is exactly the reshaping `03-CP-INVENTORY.md` and `09-DAT-WORK-SHAPE.md` describe: `SEGPLEN` bits 0-3 to `PTL` bits 28-31. **This moves the M1/M2 boundary, and `06-LEDGER.md` predicted why.** M1's premise is *"DAT off, no paging"* and that premise still holds -- the PSW has DAT disabled. But `LRA` translates **explicitly**, whatever the PSW says, so CP's 174 `TRANS` sites hit the tables during initialisation regardless. Ledger test 8 found that `LRA` behaves unexpectedly in 24-bit mode and moved AMODE 31 into M2; the same instruction now pulls the **segment-table format** into M1. So M1 cannot complete on the architecture conversion alone: it needs at least a valid ESA/390 segment-table designation and STE format for the tables CP builds for itself. **The site counts do not change; the sequencing does.** | open |
| **I-119** | A snapshot's *purpose* was implicit, so a dual-engine test IPLed CE's unconverted nucleus | A build snapshot and a test snapshot are different objects with **opposite** rules: a build snapshot is restored to reassemble from and must NOT contain a written nucleus, or it is no more bootable than the live system; a test snapshot is restored to IPL and **must** contain one, because that is the artifact under test. Nothing recorded which kind a snapshot was, so the first Hercules-4 comparison was run against `SNAP-2` -- the pre-write snapshot -- and IPLed CE's own S/370 nucleus under ESA/390. It reported `PSW=000A0000 00000017`, DMKCKP's *"error loading CKP page 2"*: a perfectly real failure of a machine nobody meant to test. The restore also destroyed the converted nucleus that had been built the night before, costing one write cycle. **Fix:** `snapshot.py` records `purpose`, validates against the opposite nucleus-write condition for each, and `check <snap> <purpose>` refuses the wrong kind. Verified both ways round on the previous night's logs -- the build log validates as `build` and is refused as `test`, the write log the reverse. | fixed |
| **I-120** | Hercules 4's `HERC_DETECT_PGMINTLOOP` terminates the **emulator**, where 3.13 stops the CPU | The first comparison run on SDL 4.9.1 exited after twelve seconds, never reached `stop`, and reported no PSW and no registers -- while 3.13 ran the full script. The cause is a *Hercules* facility rather than an architectural one: `HERC_DETECT_PGMINTLOOP`, enabled by default, ends the emulator when CP's abend re-IPL loop is detected; 3.13 merely prints `HHCCP016I Program interrupt loop` and stops the CPU. **Proved by elimination rather than guessed.** The competing hypothesis was stdin EOF, since the harness runs `</dev/null` and 4.x processes stdin as a command script: a run with **no IPL at all** survived its full twenty seconds of pauses and exited at its own `exit`, which eliminated stdin; then `facility disable HERC_DETECT_PGMINTLOOP` let the IPL run survive the full sixty seconds and execute `stop`, `psw`, `r` and `gpr`. The harness now disables it for engine 4 only, with the reason recorded at the call site. Related and recorded in the same place: 4.x reformatted the diagnostics -- PSW behind `HHC02278I`, storage behind `HHC02290I` as the containing 16-byte line, registers behind `HHC02269I` -- so storage is now requested on 16-byte boundaries and compared **by address**, and `dualdiff.py` reports **no data** as its own verdict rather than folding it into *differs*. The first dual run said `DIFF ... 4.9x: --` on every line purely because the parser was written for one engine's format; that reads like a disagreement and was not one. | fixed |
| **I-121** | The DAT table conversion is 154 sites that all assemble clean, so the symbols are renamed rather than redefined | `STE-DESIGN.md` step 1 said *"nothing executes differently yet; this makes the symbols mean the right thing"*. **Measuring it showed that to be wrong.** The page-table entry becomes a **fullword**, and CP reaches it with **13 `LH` and 5 `STH`** instructions; `LH` on a fullword field is legal assembler, so changing the DSECT alone leaves eighteen sites reading the wrong two bytes with **no diagnostic anywhere**. That is `I-116`'s shape with 303 lines to hide in instead of one. **So every symbol whose meaning moves is renamed with NO alias** -- `SEGPAGE`→`SEGPTO`, `SEGPLEN`→`SEGPTL`, `SEGINV`→`SEGINVAL`, `PAGCORE`→`PAGPFRA`, `PAGINVAL`→`PAGINV` -- and the assembler becomes the checklist: an unconverted site fails `IFO188 UNDEFINED SYMBOL` instead of quietly working on the wrong bytes. This is `19-M1-STEP2`'s reasoning reused; `INTTIO` was renamed without an alias for exactly this reason. Renaming the containers alone would not have sufficed: **17 sites reach the flags by explicit displacement** (`TM 1(R1),PAGINVAL`, `TM 3(R2),SEGINV`) and two do arithmetic on the flag value in a register (`N R10,=AL4(X'FFFFFFFF'-SEGINV)`), so only renaming the flag itself reaches them. **The sweep that produced this number had three defects of its own, each found before the number was quoted.** (1) Single-letter opcodes -- `L`, `ST`, `A`, `N` -- were unmatched by its regex, so it undercounted. (2) A line naming two fields was counted twice, inflating the total by a third. (3) It scanned only `DMK*.ASSEMBLE`, and therefore could not see `PAGTBL.MACRO`, which **generates** page tables as `DC &NP.H'8'` -- halfword entries pre-marked invalid -- nor `SHRTABLE.COPY`'s `SHRPAGE`, which is **an STE in all but name** with **59 live references** across five modules and nothing but a comment linking it to `SEGPAGE`. Fixing the coverage moved the count 238 → **303**, a 27% undercount. **Two documented items shrink.** `SEGMIG` is `X'10'`, the ESA/390 common-segment bit, and `WHAT-31BIT-NEEDS.md` lists that collision as undesigned item 2: **`SEGMIG` is referenced nowhere in the tree** and is deleted rather than converted, so half that item does not exist. `PAGTBL` likewise is **invoked nowhere**, so the generator does not need converting -- but it is recorded, because a site-generated module could start using it. Final population: **154 CHANGE, 22 REVIEW, 127 unaffected, 17 modules.** Only **19** of the 154 are in the modules that build the tables CP uses for itself (`DMKBLD` 17, `DMKCPI` 2). The earlier *"days, not weeks"* estimate was wrong and is withdrawn. | fix known |
| **I-122** | `N R1,=A(X'FFFFFE')` in `DMKBLDRL` is a 24-bit truncation wearing a flag mask's comment | Found while designing the page-table alignment, not by any sweep. `DMKBLDRL` releases a page/swap block with `L R1,SEGPAGE` / `N R1,=A(X'FFFFFE')` / `S R1,F16` / `CALL DMKFRET`, and the comment on the `N` reads *"CLEAR POSSIBLE INVALID BIT"* — which it does, `SEGINV` being `X'01'` in System/370. It also **clears bits 0-7**, so a page-table address above 16 MB is silently truncated to 24 bits before being handed to `DMKFRET`, which would then free the wrong storage. Today that cannot happen because CP's own tables live below the line, so the defect is latent and will stay latent until real storage above 16 MB is in play — which is **M5**. It appears on no list of 24-bit sites: `R01-SHIFT-SITES.md` enumerates shift constants, `dattab.py` enumerates DAT-field references, and this is neither. It is an address mask whose high byte happens to be zero in every case anyone has run. **The class it belongs to is `=A(X'00FFxxxx')`-shaped literals used as masks**, and that class has never been swept. Being fixed as a side effect rather than on its own: the page-table conversion replaces the whole recovery sequence with `L R1,PAGFREE` from a new header field, and the mask disappears with the arithmetic it supported. | fix known |
| **I-123** | `STE-DESIGN.md` claimed the alignment work had no precedent in the source; the precedent is in the same routine pair | A retracted claim, recorded because the register is only worth what it admits. §4 read *"CP allocates tables with `DMKFREE`, which returns doubleword granularity... **no existing site expresses it**, so it is the part of this work with no precedent in the source — and therefore the part to design before writing any card."* Reading `DMKBLDRT` to write that card found the idiom already there and complete: `AL R0,F7  PLUS 7 TO ENSURE 64-BYTE ALIGNMENT` / `CALL DMKFREE` / `LA R6,63(,R1)` / `N R6,=A(X'FFFFC0')` / `ST R1,SAVEWRK1` / `STH R15,VMSEGDSP`, with the matching undo in `DMKBLDRL`: `SH R1,VMSEGDSP` / `AL R0,F15  PLUS 8, PLUS 7 FOR ALIGNMENT`. **VM/370 over-allocates, rounds up, stores the displacement in the VMBLOK and reverses it on the FRET** — for the same table, in the same pair of routines, in 1979. So the segment table's conversion is a generalization of a working idiom from 64 to 4096 (`SRDL R0,8`→`12`, `F7`→`F511`, `63`→`4095`, `X'FFFFC0'`→`X'FFF000'`, `F15`→`F519`), `VMSEGDSP` is a halfword and 4088 fits it, and **the VMBLOK does not change**. Two findings came with the correction: `STC R15,VMSEG` needs no change at all, because ESA/390's STL counts 64-byte units minus one — the same units and encoding CP already writes, with only the meaning of a unit changing from 1 MB to 16 MB; and the page table, which has *no* alignment logic today, is the genuinely new part. **The pattern this shares with `I-121` and `I-105` is a confident statement about the source written without reading the source** — three instances now, each costing less than the last only because the correction arrived before the card. | fixed |
| **I-124** | Arithmetic beside a converted field names no field, so neither the assembler nor the sweep can see it | The rename in `CORE.XA0033DK` makes the assembler report every site that *names* a DAT field, and `dattab.py` reports every site a regex can find. **Both are blind to the instruction next door.** `DMKBLDRT`'s quiesce loop is the specimen: `TM SEGPAGE+3,SEGENQ+SEGINV` is flagged and swept, and two instructions later `A R1,=A(X'10000')  NEXT VIRTUAL SEGMENT ADDRESS` must become `X'100000'` because a segment is now 1 MB, while `LA R8,4(,R8)  NEXT STE ADDRESS` must stay 4 because an STE is still a fullword — **adjacent lines, one that changes and one that must not, neither naming a field.** `MVC 4(60,R6),0(R6)  DO ALL SEGTABLE` and `LA R6,64(,R6)` are the same shape: 60 and 64 are a 16-entry chunk expressed as literals. There is no mechanical detector for this class, because the thing that identifies it is what the register is *for*, not what it is *called*. The defence is procedural and bounded: **read the whole basic block around every flagged site**, and the flagged sites cluster in 16 modules. Recorded separately from `I-121` because the mitigation is different — `I-121` is closed by a rename, this one cannot be. | open |
| **I-125** | `PAGTBL.MACRO` generates page tables the DSECT no longer describes — `I-116`'s shape, fourth instance | `dattab.py`'s non-module sites turned up two in `PAGTBL.MACRO`: `DC &NP.H'8'`, a generator that emits `&NP` **halfword** entries preset to `X'0008'`, which is `PAGINVAL` at System/370's bit 12. `CORE.COPY` declares `PAGCORE DS 1H` and the macro emits `H'8'`; convert the DSECT to a fullword and the macro keeps emitting halfwords of `0008`, which as fullword PTEs read as page-frame address `X'0008'` with the invalid bit **clear**. It assembles clean, because `DC H'8'` is legal whatever a DSECT elsewhere says. This is exactly `I-116` — a DSECT and the generator that builds its storage, in different files, with nothing joining them — after `RDEVICE.MACRO`/`RBLOKS` (`I-116`), `SHRTABLE.COPY`'s undeclared STE twin (`I-121`), and now this. The correct target is `DC &NP.F'1024'` — `X'00000400'`, `PAGINV` at ESA/390 bit 21 — and the generator must change in the same deck as the DSECT, not a later one. **Standing rule, now stated for the fourth time: a field's declaration and the code that generates that field's storage are one change.** The only reason this one was found before it cost anything is that `dattab.py` was extended to scan `.COPY` and `.MACRO` files, which it did not do in its first version. | fix known |
| **I-126** | `LA Rn,0(,Rn)` is CP's strip-the-flags idiom in **215 sites across 79 modules**, and AMODE 31 turns every one into a no-op | Found by reading `DMKBLD`'s VIRT=REAL builder to write its conversion cards, not by any sweep: `L R8,SEGPAGE` / `LA R8,0(,R8)  CLEAR OUT NUMBER OF PAGES`. The idiom works in System/370 because a packed pointer keeps its flags in the high byte and `LA` zeroes bits 0-7. **In ESA/390 what `LA` clears depends on AMODE** — bits 0-7 in AMODE 24, **bit 0 only in AMODE 31** — so in 31-bit mode the instruction strips nothing and the flag bits remain in the address. Counted across `DMK*.ASSEMBLE`: **215 self-strip sites in 79 modules, 152 of them carrying a comment that says strip, clear, flag, length or without.** Three properties make this the worst-shaped item found so far. **It is invisible in M1** — CP runs AMODE 24 through a clean IPL, so all 215 behave exactly as today and no diagnostic exists. **It breaks simultaneously in M2** — the instant the PSW goes AMODE 31, 215 strips become no-ops in 79 modules at once, which is the hardest possible shape to bisect. And **it is in none of the existing enumerations**: `R01-SHIFT-SITES.md` counts shift constants, `dattab.py` counts DAT-field references, the 358 figure counts format-sensitive table accesses — this is an *instruction idiom*, and no sweep in this project looks for idioms. The longhand relatives are the same family and far smaller, about 25 sites: `=X'00FFFFF8'` (5), `=X'00FFFFF0'` (3), `=X'00FFFFC0'` (3), `=X'00FF0000'` (3), plus `I-122`'s `=A(X'FFFFFE')`. **Deliberately not being fixed now.** It changes nothing for a clean IPL, and converting 215 sites before CP can boot is the wrong order. It is recorded against **M2**, whose estimate was made without it. | open |
| **I-127** | The swap-table header is 8 bytes, not 12: a field list read without its `ORG` | A small arithmetic error, corrected within the hour, recorded because of how it was made. Computing the cost of 256-entry page tables needed `SWPTABLE`'s header size, and reading the field list gave `SWPVM DS 1F` / `SWPFLAG2 DS 1X` / `SWPPAG DS 1F` / `SWPFLAG DS 1X` — from which `SWPFLAG-SWPVM` looks like 12, since a `DS 1F` after a byte aligns to 8. The line between them is `ORG SWPFLAG2`, which puts the location counter back to 4, so `SWPPAG` is at 4 and the header is **8**. `DMKBLDRT`'s own `LA R9,PAGCORE+16*2+8  ADDRESS OF 1ST SWAPTABLE` states it plainly and was read without being believed. Consequences: `PAGBMP` is exactly **192** bytes today — 24 doublewords with no remainder — and exactly **3104** after conversion, 388 doublewords. The published 3% cost figure survives unchanged (3160 bytes per megabyte against 3072), and a second claim does not: the trailing `+8` in `PAGBMP`'s `EQU` was described as slack absorbing a truncating `SRL R0,3`, and there is no truncation to absorb in either architecture. **The lesson is the specific one that a `grep` of declarations is not a DSECT**: `ORG`, `CNOP` and alignment all move fields, and the generated offset is the only authority. The same mistake on a field that mattered would have been `I-116` again. | fixed |
| **I-128** | System/370 hides packed fields in the **ignored** high byte of an address; ESA/390 moves them to the low bits, which are never ignored | **The structural law behind `I-122`, `I-124`, `I-126` and most of the 67 silent sites, and the item that changes the size of M1.** Found writing `DMKBLD`'s deck, at `L R6,VMSEG` / `LA R6,0(R3,R6)  POINT TO 1ST STE` with no mask anywhere. That is correct System/370: `VMSEG` holds the segment-table length in bits 0-7 and the origin in bits 8-25, and **24-bit address formation ignores bits 0-7 of a base register**, so the packed word is usable as an address with nothing to strip. The same trick appears in `DMKPTR`'s `L R3,VMSEG` / `CLM R9,B'0111',1(R3)` -- a base register still carrying its length byte. **ESA/390 puts STL in bits 25-31, STE flags in 26-31 and PTE flags in 21-22 -- all low bits, none ignored by address formation.** So every packed pointer that worked by living in the ignored high byte now needs an explicit mask where it needed none, and **this is live in M1**, unlike `I-126`, which sleeps until AMODE 31: the bits that stop being ignored are the low ones and AMODE 24 does not help. Measured for `VMSEG` alone: **92 `L`/`LCTL` sites across 40 modules**; of the 30 `L Rn,VMSEG` sites classified, **16 unmasked and needing a mask added**, **14 masked with a constant that must change** -- `DMKVAT`'s is explicit, `CLRBITS DC X'00FFFFF8'  MASK TO CLEAR FLAG BITS - STE`, which becomes `X'7FFFFFC0'` -- and **9 `IC Rn,VMSEG`** that read the length from byte 0 and must read byte 3 masked to seven bits. `LCTL C1,C1,VMSEG` is the only form that is correct as written, because the hardware wants the whole designation. **None of these are flagged by the assembler, because `VMSEG` is not renamed.** The remedy is the one that worked for the DAT fields: **rename `VMSEG` to `VMSTD` with no alias**, so the assembler enumerates all 105 references instead of leaving them to a regex. §2 of the design doc said of `VMSEG` that *"both halves move"* and then listed only `DMKBLDRT`'s two instructions; the 90 readers were never counted. | fix known |
| **I-129** | `STC R15,VMSEG` does need changing: the value is unchanged, the target byte moves from 0 to 3 | A retracted claim from `STE-DESIGN.md` §4a, and the fourth error in that document of exactly one shape. It read: *"**`STC R15,VMSEG` needs no change at all.** ESA/390's STL is 'length in units of 64 bytes, minus one' -- the same units and the same encoding CP already writes."* The encoding claim is correct and the conclusion does not follow. System/370 puts the length in **bits 0-7**, so `ST R6,VMSEG` followed by `STC R15,VMSEG` writes it to byte 0. ESA/390's STL is **bits 25-31**, which is byte **3**. So the instruction becomes `STC R15,VMSEG+3`, and it is safe there precisely because the segment table is 4096-aligned, making bits 20-31 of the stored address zero and leaving the S bit (24) clear. **The error was conflating "the value is unchanged" with "the instruction is unchanged."** Four instances now in one document, all the same shape -- a confident statement about the source or the architecture written one step short of the instruction it governs: the alignment precedent (`I-123`), the safe first step (`I-121`), the swap-table header (`I-127`), and this. The procedural answer is in `I-124`: read the whole basic block, because `VMSEG` is not renamed and no diagnostic can reach this line. | fixed |
| **I-130** | The page-table header's 16-byte offset is a literal in seven places, and reordering the DSECT would have broken all seven silently | Caught by grepping for the constant before making a design change that depended on it. The page/swap block needs 64-byte alignment, so the release path can no longer recover the `DMKFREE` address with `S R1,F16`. The elegant fix looks like **reordering `PAGTABLE` so the page table sits at the block origin** -- then the FRET recovers the block from the STE with no arithmetic and no new field. Searching for what assumes *block origin = header* found seven sites that say so in a literal, six in `DMKATS` and one in `DMKBLD`: `S R2,F16  BACKUP TO HEADER` (00294000, 00340000), `S R6,F16  BACKUP TO HEADER` (00500000), `A R6,F16  RESET TO PTO` (00510000), `A R1,F16  LOAD ADDR PTO` (00613000), `S R5,F16  BACKUP TO PAGTABLE HEADER` (00719000), `S R1,F16  BACK-UP TO START OF CORE FOR TABLES` (00632600). **Not one of them names a DAT field, so the assembler cannot flag any of them** -- `I-124`'s class, and `I-116`'s shape a fifth time. The reorder is rejected. But the finding reframes the work: those seven are wrong **either way**, because the header's offset changes whatever layout is chosen, so the question is not which layout is cheaper but which makes them visible. The answer is to stop writing the offset as a number -- `S R2,=A(PAGPFRA-PAGSTMP)` -- which turns seven invisible sites into seven the assembler maintains. The `DMKFREE` address, the one thing rounding really destroys, goes in a new header fullword used only by the release path. Two constraints found while checking: **`STCK PAGSTMP` stores eight bytes and needs a doubleword-aligned operand**, so the new field cannot go first (and `STCK` therefore clobbers `PAGACT` and `PAGTOT`, which the following `XC PAGACT(12),PAGACT` cleans up -- deliberate, and invisible in the DSECT); and **`F16` means two different things in `DMKBLD`** -- the header size at 00632600 and pages-per-segment at 00373000, `S R5,F16  SUBTRACT 16 FROM NUMBER OF PAGES`, which must become 256. Same spelling, same module, opposite conversions. | fix known |
| **I-131** | Build logs are the project's measurements and every run overwrote the last one | `build.sh` names its log after the verb -- `f1.log`, `q1.log`, `n1.log`, `t1.log`, `d3.log`, `d4.log` -- so each `full` replaced the previous `full`'s log. That looked harmless while a log was a transcript. It is not: **`asmerr.py` and `deckchk.py` both take a log as their input**, so the log IS the measurement. The 190-diagnostic log that established the `176 flagged / 176 predicted / 0 holes` reconciliation was destroyed by the very next build, which was started to verify the decks that reconciliation authorised. Nothing was lost in practice -- the new log is a superset, carrying the same 139 remaining sites plus the two converted modules -- but the historical record of the result is gone, and `deckchk.py` would have silently measured a deck against the wrong log if the overwrite had happened mid-analysis. Fixed in `run()`, which now moves an existing log to `logs/<verb>-<mtime>.log` before starting, so the archive is keyed by when the run happened rather than by when it was read. The guard belongs in `run()` rather than in each verb because `run()` is the one place that opens the file. **The general shape: a tool that reads an artifact makes that artifact evidence, and evidence cannot live at a fixed path that something else writes.** Same family as `I-111` (a stale snapshot indistinguishable from a current one) and `I-119` (a test run against the pre-write snapshot) -- state identified by position rather than by content. | fixed |
| **I-132** | CP packs addresses into **three bytes** in 278 sites across 81 modules, and a three-byte field has nowhere to put a 31-bit address | Found by accident: scanning the unconverted modules for `I-124`-class silent sites, the most common pattern turned out not to be a DAT issue at all. `STCM R8,B'0111',CORSWPNT+1  STORE ADDR. OF SWAP ENTRY` -- `ICM`/`STCM`/`CLM` with mask `B'0111'` is CP's idiom for moving an address in and out of a **three-byte** field, which is 24 bits by construction. Counted across the tree: **278 sites in 81 modules**, led by `DMKPTR` 22, `DMKVDE` 14, `DMKVDD` 12, `DMKIOH` 10, `DMKPER` 9. **Only 12 of the fields involved are declared `AL3`/`XL3`**; the rest are three bytes of a wider field reached by displacement, which is why no DSECT sweep finds them and why `I-116`'s generator-versus-declaration check would not either. Like `I-126` this is invisible through M1 and M2 -- a 24-bit field is entirely adequate while every address CP handles is below 16 MB -- and it becomes live at **M5**, real storage above the line. **Unlike `I-126` it cannot be fixed by changing an instruction.** A three-byte field has no room for a 31-bit address, so each of the 278 is a *structure* change, in CP's core control blocks, with the generator-and-declaration pairing problem of `I-116` and `I-125` attached to every one of them. This is a materially different answer to "what is between here and 31-bit" than `WHAT-31BIT-NEEDS.md` currently gives, and it belongs on M5's ledger with a number before M5 is estimated again. | open |
| **I-133** | The remaining DAT conversion is about 229 changes, measured -- and `DMKBLD`'s ratio would have overstated it by a third | Recorded as a calibration point, because this project's estimates have been wrong in both directions and the method matters more than the number. `DMKBLD` came out at **37 flagged sites and 46 silent ones**, a silent-to-flagged ratio of 1.24; extrapolating that across the 139 remaining flagged sites gives about 310 changes. Measuring instead -- scanning the 14 unconverted modules for the specific patterns `DMKBLD` taught, counting only occurrences within four lines of a named DAT field -- gives **about 90 silent candidates**, for roughly **229 remaining changes**. The ratio overstates because `DMKBLD` is the *builder*: it carries the allocation and alignment arithmetic that the other modules only consume, so its silent work is unrepresentative of theirs. Concentration: `DMKPGS` 30, `DMKCFG` 16, `DMKPTR` 16, `DMKATS` 11, then a long tail of ones and twos. **Two honest caveats.** These are candidates, not confirmed sites -- each needs reading, and the count will move in both directions. And the scan only knows the patterns already learned: `DMKBLD` produced two that no rule would have predicted (`S R9,F4` reaching `PAGSWP` through an assumed four-byte gap, and a single `SRL R1,8` serving both a CORTABLE index and a page-table entry because the two strides happened to agree), so the next module should be expected to produce another. The estimate is a floor on the reading, not a ceiling on the work. | open |
| **I-134** | The page-table header's offset is a literal in **33** places, not 7, and classifying them by comment keyword got two wrong | `I-130` found seven `S Rn,F16  BACKUP TO HEADER` sites and concluded the header offset was a literal in seven places. `block.py` turned up another in `DMKPGS` while reading a basic block, so the literal was counted across the tree: **48 `S/A Rn,F16` sites in 18 modules**, of which **33 are the page-table header** -- `DMKATS` 9, `DMKPGS` 7, `DMKCFG` 6, `DMKPTR` 3, `DMKVMA` 3, `DMKCPP` 2, `DMKBLD` 1, `DMKCPU` 1, `DMKVAT` 1 -- with 9 belonging to `RCWTASK`, 1 to a CORTABLE entry, and 1 (`DMKBLD` 00373000) being pages-per-segment, which converts the opposite way. **The first classification was by comment keyword and got two modules wrong**: `DMKUNT` 00221100 reads `BACK OFF 16 TO 1ST HEADER STA...` and is an `RCWTASK` header, and both `DMKCPB` sites likewise. Re-classifying by *context* -- whether a DAT field appears within five lines of the register being adjusted -- is what produced the correct list, and the error is the same one that cost two defects in `block.py` an hour earlier: **writing the test for the instances already seen rather than for the property that matters.** Consequence for the design: `PAGFREE` moves `PAGPFRA` from offset 16 to 24, so all 33 must become `S Rn,=A(PAGCORE-PAGSTMP)`. Only `DMKVAT` is outside the 16 modules already in scope, so the marginal cost is one module and 33 identical substitutions. **The substitution is right on its own terms** whatever happens to `PAGFREE`, which is `I-130`'s argument applied to 33 sites instead of 7. | fix known |
| **I-135** | The page-table alignment gap is provably unrecoverable, so storing it is necessary rather than convenient | Recorded because the cheap alternative was attempted and shown impossible, and a design decision that survived an attempt to avoid it is worth more than one that was never challenged. For the gap between what `DMKFREE` returns and the `PAGTABLE` header to be a known constant, `DMKFREE`'s address would need a fixed residue mod 64; it is first-fit from a free-storage chain and `DMKFRE`'s documented contract promises doubleword granularity and nothing else, so it has no fixed residue. Rounding down, rounding to the next boundary rather than the first at-or-after, and over-allocating head room each reduce to needing `A & 63`, which is exactly what the rounding discarded. **The one genuine alternative is to `DMKFRET` the leading slack back immediately**, which costs two extra free-storage calls per page table and fragments the chain with 0-56 byte holes -- rejected on cost, not on correctness. A second candidate location was rejected on correctness: the spare doubleword at `PAGBMP-8` looked free and would have left `PAGPFRA` at offset 16, saving all 33 sites of `I-134`, until a grep showed **`PAGBMP` is a stride, not just a size** -- `LA R6,PAGBMP(,R6)  BUMP TO ATTACHED PAGTABLE`, `MVC PAGTABLE(PAGBMP),0(R10)  COPY PAGE&SWAP - CPU`, `DCPAGBMP DC A(PAGBMP)` -- so two blocks sit back to back for AP support and one is copied from the other wholesale. A `DMKFREE` address stored there would be copied into the second block, giving it the first block's address. AP=NO hides that today (`I-50`), and relying on a configuration flag to hide a wrong value is what `I-116` was about. | fixed |
| **I-136** | `PAGFREE` collided with a 1979 label in `DMKPTR`, and the tool written to catch it reported 17 collisions where there is 1 | The new `PAGTABLE` header field was named `PAGFREE`. `DMKPTR` has had `PAGFREE  EQU   *  ENTRY FROM WITHIN DMKPTRAN` since 1979 -- a subroutine that gets a free page, in the paging module, where *PAG* plus *FREE* is the obvious name for both things. `CORE.COPY` is copied by **42 modules**, so the field lands in all of them and the assembler said `IFO196 PAGFREE HAS BEEN PREVIOUSLY DEFINED` **once**, in a list of 147 diagnostics, in the single module that collides. Renamed to **`PAGORIG`**; the next candidate tried, `PAGFRET`, would have collided in `DMKVAT`. **Two process failures, each worse than the name.** First, `asmerr.py` filed the diagnostic under *"NOT about this conversion"* -- a collision with a symbol the conversion **introduced** is as much ours as a diagnostic gets, and whose diagnostic it is cannot be a judgement call. Fixed with an `INTRODUCED` set and a `COLLIDED` verdict of its own, because filing it under `MISSED` would read as a hole in `dattab.py`, which does not look for collisions and should not. Second, `symchk.py` was written to find collisions before a build rather than after, and **its first run reported 17 where there is 1**: it compared symbol names tree-wide, when assembler symbols are per-assembly -- `DMKCKP` and `DMKCPI` may both define `SNSIO` and neither cares. What makes `PAGFREE` real is its *scope*: a deck on a MODULE reaches that module and the members it copies, a deck on a MEMBER reaches every module that copies it. **The tool committed the error it exists to catch, reporting by name rather than by scope**, and shipping the count would have been `blocksize.py` again -- *"a tool that reports three defects where there is one would have sent the next reader to fix RCHBLOK"*. With the scope rule it reports exactly one, and after the rename, none. | fixed |
| **I-137** | `asmchk` had two defects that cancelled into a plausible failure, and the surviving one would have built a nucleus from object decks full of zeros | The worst-shaped defect found today, because its failure mode is a corrupt nucleus rather than lost time, and because **the cancellation is what let it live**. At the end of the 1 October verification build `asmchk` printed `### MISSING: ['DMKATS', 'DMKCFG', 'DMKCFH', 'DMKCPP', 'DMKPGS']` and exited 1 -- five modules, non-zero status, the look of a check doing its job. Both halves are wrong. **(1)** All five produced an object deck. `asmchk` knew `TEXT CREATED` and `TXTLCL CREATED` but not **`TXTHRC CREATED`**, which is the output name when a module's highest update level is HRC -- most modules, since only the ones this project patches carry an LCL deck. Five false positives. **(2)** Eleven modules with real errors were reported benign. The rule was *"flagged but output created: benign"*, correct for `I-34`'s four 3375/3390 MNOTEs and catastrophic for `IFO188 UNDEFINED SYMBOL`, because **assembler XF emits the instruction with the symbol resolved as zero**: `L R3,SEGPAGE` becomes `L R3,0`, `DMKPTR TXTLCL CREATED` appears, and the deck is a lie. Measured on that log: `DMKPTR` 48 diagnostics, `DMKPGS` 42, `DMKATS` 37, 197 across 11 modules, **every one of which produced an object deck**. `build.sh write` would have VMFLOADed them into a nucleus that IPLs and fails with no relationship to the cause. **The two defects hid each other**: the false positives produced a non-zero exit and a plausible complaint, so the false negatives never had to be explained. A check that fails for the wrong reason is worse than one that does not fail, because it looks like it is working. Rewritten to test **severity** rather than the existence of output -- an MNOTE prints `IFO197 *** MNOTE ***`, anything else makes the deck untrustworthy whether or not it exists -- and to name the diagnostics per module with their codes. On the same log it now reports no module missing a deck and lists the 11. Seventh instance today of a check that cannot fail, after `I-111`, the `mkrun` fall-through, `I-112`, `I-115`, `I-117` and `I-131`. | fixed |
| **I-138** | `aux()` replaced an AUXLCL instead of merging, so a second deck on the same module was silently never applied | `DMKVMA` already had `XA0030DK` (the ECPS no-op) written by a loop at the end of `main()`. Adding the DAT deck `XA0036DK` in a new loop **before** it meant the later loop rewrote `DMKVMA.AUXLCL` with only its own entry, and **the AUXLCL is what `VMFASM` reads** -- a deck missing from it is a deck that never runs, so the module would have failed to assemble with all 13 of the sites the deck exists to fix. `build.py`'s own comment warned about exactly this (*"Written after the per-module sections above, so the two modules that already have an AUXLCL get BOTH decks listed"*) and the warning did not survive a new per-module section being added above it. **`deckchk.py` cannot see this**, because it globs the deck *files* -- it reported `DECKS XA0030DK XA0036DK` for a module whose AUXLCL listed one. Cost would have been a 35-minute build and a confusing result: a module failing with its full original site count after its deck was written and verified. Two fixes, because the comment was already the first attempt at the first one. `aux()` now **merges**, newest first, a repeated identifier updating its description in place; and `auxcheck()` asserts the invariant separately -- every generated `*.XA*DK` must be listed in its member's AUXLCL -- so a future generator cannot break it quietly. The order the two end up in does not matter here: the decks' anchors are disjoint, 00172000 against 00133000-00518000. | fixed |
| **I-139** | `DMKCFG`'s attached-processor shared-segment path is deliberately left unconverted, with an `ABEND` | A scope decision recorded rather than hidden. `DMKCFG` 01024000-01025000 copies a page+swap block to the attached processor's: `MVC PAGTABLE(PAGBMP),0(R10)` and `MVC 0(PAGBMP,R14),0(R10)`. `PAGBMP` is 3136 bytes now and `MVC` stops at 256, so the assembler rejects both (`IFO224 LENGTH ERROR`) -- they must become `MVCL`, which needs an even-odd register pair, and **R0, R1, R5, R7, R10 and R14 are all live across those two cards**. Inventing a register allocation and a save area for code that **cannot execute** -- `XA0014DK` forces `AP=NO` on purpose, `I-50` -- and therefore cannot be tested, is how latent defects get written with confidence. So the two cards become `ABEND 9  AP SHARED SEGS NOT CONVERTED`: it assembles, it is unreachable, and if anyone revives AP it fails immediately and says why instead of corrupting two page tables. The surrounding AP code IS converted, because it must assemble; only the copy is stubbed. **Two related things found while deciding this.** `LA R0,PAGBMP+PAGBMP` (01056500) is 6272 and an `LA` displacement stops at 4095, so it becomes a literal load -- the assembler caught that one itself with `IFO208`. And the AP path was what forced `PAGBMP` to a multiple of 64 at all: `SWLENGTH` is the stride to the second page table, so a `PAGBMP` of 3112 would have put it off a 64-byte boundary. The stub does not remove that requirement, because `DMKVMA` and `DMKATS` use the same stride outside this routine. | open |
| **I-109** | `DMKDMP` takes the dump with `ISK`, so CP cannot report why it abended | With `I-108` fixed the ESA/390 IPL runs past device initialisation and CP **speaks for the first time**: `DMKDMP908I SYSTEM FAILURE; CODE PRG018 PROCESSOR 00`, immediately followed by `DMKDMP905W SYSTEM DUMP FAILURE; PROGRAM CHECK`. The second message explains itself: `r 8C` gives `00020001` -- **ILC 1**, so a two-byte instruction -- and at `52084` sits `0934`, **`ISK R3,R4`**, one of the twelve System/370 instructions 370-XA withdrew (`SA22-7201-08` Appendix F marks it `-`). `DMKDMP` has **four** `ISK` sites (00412000, 00500000, 00505000, 01054000) and they are now on the critical path, because they sit in the abend handler. This puts a second storage-key site on the Stage-1 path alongside `I-104`'s `SSK`: two of the 67 sites gate the IPL while the other 65 remain correctly deferred, which is the point `I-104` made about asking of any deferred axis *which single site runs earliest*. **Order of work: fix the dump first, because it is the instrument.** `PRG018`'s own cause is still unknown -- it happened before the dump, and the program-check data was overwritten by the dump's own failure -- so converting these four `ISK`s to `ISKE` is what makes the next question askable at all. Same reasoning as converting `DMKOPR` before chasing the next wall, which paid for itself within a day. **Fixed 30 Sep**, four cards, one per site, and checked against `SA22-7201-08` rather than assumed: `ISKE` inserts *"the seven-bit storage key in bit positions 24-30 of general register R1, and bit 31 is set to zero"* with bits 0-23 unchanged -- the same result format `ISK` produces, so every following `STC` is unaffected. (Worth stating because the facing page's `IVSK` returns only ACC and F in bits 24-28, and a two-column PDF extract interleaves them.) The operand differs only in granularity, so all four 2 KB-stepping loops now store each page's key twice, which is the truth under the 4 KB keys CE already runs with -- redundant, not wrong, exactly `I-104`'s finding. Class closed mechanically rather than by reading: the `s370only` sweep finds 26 S/370-only sites in `DMKDMP` and all 26 fall inside this deck's ranges, so the module reaches zero. Knowing deviation recorded in the deck: Hercules marks `ISKE` `GENx370x390x900` and accepts it in S/370 mode, but `ISKE` arrived with 370-XA and a real S/370 would not, so the cards are unconditional with no architecture probe. **And `PRG018` is now known -- see `I-110`, which the fix made askable.** | fixed |
| **I-61** | A deck cannot anchor on a sequence number it generates itself | `XA0004DK` placed the DMKIOS shims with `./ I 02651150`, taking 02651150 from the sequences its *own* earlier `./ I 02651100` generates. `UPDATE` applies every control card against the **input** file in one forward pass, so 02651150 does not exist yet and the insert silently did nothing — the assembly then failed with `IFO188 IOSXTIO IS AN UNDEFINED SYMBOL` seven times, which points at the call sites rather than at the missing definitions. Fixed by folding the shims into the same `./ I 02651100` insert, which also keeps them in one readable block. The general rule: **every anchor must be a record that exists before the deck is applied**, and `next_seq` reads the base file precisely so that it cannot suggest a generated one. | fixed |
| **I-33** | Long unconditional pauses in a Hercules `.rc` cannot be shortened | The `ASMDMK` run padded `pause 3600` twice plus `pause 900` for an assembly estimated at 100 minutes; it took 90 seconds, and Hercules then sat idle for two hours before reaching `/cp shutdown`. With no panel tty, no `HTTPPORT` in the config and only a 3270 port, there is no way in. Killing the process is the one thing that must not happen. **Size pauses to the work and poll the log from the host side**, which loses nothing because the log is continuously readable. | fixed |
| **I-34** | `DMKRIO` flags 4 MNOTEs for 3375/3390 | `RDEVICE ADDRESS=(170,16),DEVTYPE=3375` and `DEVTYPE=3390` produce `4,UNSUPPORTED DEVICE TYPE` — CE's site configuration declares DASD the `RDEVICE` macro does not know. Severity 4, and **`DMKRIO TEXT CREATED`**, so a nucleus can still link; `VMFASM` reports it as an error only because the return code is non-zero. Pre-existing, nothing to do with the conversion. | documented |
| **I-35** | `DMKBTS` has no source — and CE says so itself | Reported first as a gap, then **retracted** when Mike pointed to `systems/vmce/source/cp/DMKBTS.ASSEMBLE`. The retraction was the error. `maintenance/source-catalog.json` carries the module's own entry: `{"name": "DMKBTS", "filetype": "TEXT", "source": null, "bootstrap": "194/DMKBTS.TEXT", "status": "bootstrap-only"}`. It is the **only CP module in the nucleus with no source at all** — 266 of CE's 276 nucleus inputs are `source-found`, and of the ten that are not, nine are loaders, terminators and `SLC` bootstrap records. So the original claim was correct, the tree is complete, and `DMKBTS` ships as an object deck. See `R-24` for what that costs the conversion. | closed |
